Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — transfer of energy as heat between the system and its surroundings; if the whole wall is moved by the application of force from the surroundings, without change of
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — volume of the wall, so as to change the volume of the system, then it is also at the same time transferring energy as work. A chemical reaction within a system can lead
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — to electrical long-range forces and to electric current ﬂow, which transfer energy as work between system and surroundings, though the system's chemical reactions
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — themselves (except for the special limiting case in which in they are driven through devices in the surroundings so as to occur along a line of thermodynamic
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — equilibrium) are always irreversible and do not directly interact with the surroundings of the system.[38]
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — Non-mechanical work contrasts with pressure–volume work. Pressure–volume work is one of the two mainly considered kinds of mechanical contact work. A force acts
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — on the interfacing wall between system and surroundings. e force is due to the pressure exerted on the interfacing wall by the material inside the system; that
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — pressure is an internal state variable of the system, but is properly measured by external devices at the wall. e work is due to change of system volume by expansion
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — or contraction of the system. If the system expands, in the present article it is said to do positive work on the surroundings. If the system contracts, in the present article
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — it is said to do negative work on the surroundings. Pressure–volume work is a kind of contact work, because it occurs through direct material contact with the
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — surrounding wall or maer at the boundary of the system. It is accurately described by changes in state variables of the system, such as the time courses of changes in
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — the pressure and volume of the system. e volume of the system is classiﬁed as a "deformation variable", and is properly measured externally to the system, in the
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — surroundings. Pressure–volume work can have either positive or negative sign. Pressure–volume work, performed slowly enough, can be made to approach the ﬁctive
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — reversible quasi-static ideal.
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — Non-mechanical work also contrasts with sha work. Sha work is the other of the two mainly considered kinds of mechanical contact work. It transfers energy by
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — rotation, but it does not eventually change the shape or volume of the system. Because it does not change the volume of the system it is not measured as pressure–
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — volume work, and it is called isochoric work. Considered solely in terms of the eventual diﬀerence between initial and ﬁnal shapes and volumes of the system, sha
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — work does not make a change. During the process of sha work, for example the rotation of a paddle, the shape of the system changes cyclically, but this does not make
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — an eventual change in the shape or volume of the system. Sha work is a kind of contact work, because it occurs through direct material contact with the surrounding
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — maer at the boundary of the system. A system that is initially in a state of thermodynamic equilibrium cannot initiate any change in its internal energy. In particular, it
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — cannot initiate sha work. is explains the curious use of the phrase "inanimate material agency" by Kelvin in one of his statements of the second law of
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — thermodynamics. ermodynamic operations or changes in the surroundings are considered to be able to create elaborate changes such as indeﬁnitely prolonged,
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — varied, or ceased rotation of a driving sha, while a system that starts in a state of thermodynamic equilibrium is inanimate and cannot spontaneously do that.[39] us
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — the sign of sha work is always negative, work being done on the system by the surroundings. Sha work can hardly be done indeﬁnitely slowly; consequently it
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — always produces entropy within the system, because it relies on friction or viscosity within the system for its transfer.[40] e foregoing comments about sha work
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — apply only when one ignores that the system can store angular momentum and its related energy.

Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — distribution of the maer within the system. e system gains internal energy (or other relevant cardinal quantity of energy, such as enthalpy) through internal friction.
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — As seen by the surroundings, such frictional work appears as mechanical work done on the system, but as seen by the system, it appears as transfer of energy as heat.
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — When the system is in its own state of internal thermodynamic equilibrium, its temperature is uniform throughout. If the volume and other extensive state variables,
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — apart from entropy, are held constant over the process, then the transferred heat must appear as increased temperature and entropy; in a uniform gravitational ﬁeld, the
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — pressure of the system will be greater at the boom than at the top.
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — By deﬁnition, the relevant cardinal energy function is distinct from the gravitational potential energy of the system as a whole; the laer may also change as a result of
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — gravitational work done by the surroundings on the system. e gravitational potential energy of the system is a component of its total energy, alongside its other
Work (thermodynamics) - Wikipedia — components, namely its cardinal thermodynamic (e.g. internal) energy and its kinetic energy as a whole system in motion.

Creative work - Wikipedia — Creative work
Creative work - Wikipedia — A creative work is a manifestation of creative eﬀort in the world through a creative process involving one or more individuals. e term includes ﬁne artwork
Creative work - Wikipedia — (sculpture, paintings, drawing, sketching, performance art), dance, writing (literature), ﬁlmmaking, and musical composition. Creative works require a creative mindset
Creative work - Wikipedia — and are not typically rendered in an arbitrary fashion, although works may demonstrate (i.e., have in common) a degree of arbitrariness, such that it is improbable that
Creative work - Wikipedia — two people would independently create the same work.
Creative work - Wikipedia — At its base, creative work involves two main steps – having an idea, and then turning that idea into a substantive form or process. Typically, the creative process results
Creative work - Wikipedia — in work that has some aesthetic value, identiﬁed as a creative expression. Naturally, this expression generally invokes external stimuli (e.g., inﬂuences and experiences)
Creative work - Wikipedia — which a person draws on because they view the source as creative or inspirational; the degree to which this is reﬂected may be used in determinations of the
Creative work - Wikipedia — derivativeness of the created work. Alternatively, the creator may draw on imagination, and their references may be clouded even to them, for the nature of imagination
Creative work - Wikipedia — is as yet not fully understood philosophically, and the level of necessary self-examination of an artist's internal processing is a challenge for even those most self-aware
Creative work - Wikipedia — of their minds and mental processes.
Creative work - Wikipedia — e term is frequently used in the context of copyright.[1]
Creative work - Wikipedia — For the purpose of section 221(2)(c) of the Income Tax (Trading and Other Income) Act 2005, the expression "creative works" means:
Creative work - Wikipedia — (a) literary, dramatic, musical or artistic works, or
Creative work - Wikipedia — (b) designs,
Creative work - Wikipedia — created by the taxpayer personally or, if the qualifying trade, profession or vocation is carried on in partnership, by one or more of the partners personally.[2]

Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity is the ability to form novel and valuable ideas or works using one's imagination. Products of creativity may be intangible
Creativity - Wikipedia — (e.g. an idea, scientiﬁc theory, literary work, musical composition, or joke), or a physical object (e.g. an invention, dish or meal, piece of
Creativity - Wikipedia — jewelry, costume, a painting).
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity may also describe the ability to ﬁnd new solutions to problems, or new methods to accomplish a goal. erefore, creativity
Creativity - Wikipedia — enables people to solve problems in new ways.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Most ancient cultures (including Ancient Greece, Ancient China, and Ancient India) lacked the concept of creativity, seeing art as a
Creativity - Wikipedia — form of discovery rather than a form of creation. In the Judeo-Christian-Islamic tradition, creativity was seen as the sole province of
Creativity - Wikipedia — God, and human creativity was considered an expression of God's work; the modern conception of creativity came about during the
Creativity - Wikipedia — Renaissance, inﬂuenced by humanist ideas.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Scholarly interest in creativity is found in a number of disciplines, primarily psychology, business studies, and cognitive science. It is
Creativity - Wikipedia — also present in education and the humanities (including philosophy and the arts).
Creativity - Wikipedia — e English word "creativity" comes from the Latin terms creare (meaning "to create"). Its derivational suﬃxes also come from Latin. e word "create" appeared in
Creativity - Wikipedia — English as early as the 14th century—notably in Chaucer's e Parson's Tale[1] to indicate divine creation.[2] e modern meaning of creativity in reference to human
Creativity - Wikipedia — creation did not emerge until aer the Age of Enlightenment.
Creativity - Wikipedia — In a summary of scientiﬁc research into creativity, psychology professor Michael Mumford wrote, "We seem to have reached a general agreement that creativity
Creativity - Wikipedia — involves the production of novel, useful products."[3] In psychologist Robert Sternberg's words, creativity produces "something original and worthwhile".[4]

Creativity - Wikipedia — diﬀerent deﬁnitions can be found in literature.[5] One deﬁnition given by Dr. E. Paul Torrance in the context of assessing an individual's creative ability is "a process of

Creativity - Wikipedia — Philosophy professor Ignacio L. Götz, following the etymology of the word, argued that creativity is not necessarily "making". He conﬁned it to the act of creating
Creativity - Wikipedia — without thinking about the end product.[7] While many deﬁnitions of creativity seem almost synonymous with originality, Götz also emphasized the diﬀerence between
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity and originality. Götz asserted that one can be creative without necessarily being original. When someone creates something, they are certainly creative at that
Creativity - Wikipedia — point, but they may not be original in the sense that their creation is not something new. However, originality and creativity can go hand-in-hand.[7]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity in general is usually distinguished from innovation in particular, where the emphasis is on implementation. Academics and authors Teresa Amabile and
Creativity - Wikipedia — Michael Pra deﬁned creativity as the production of novel and useful ideas and innovation as the implementation of creative ideas,[8] while the OECD and Eurostat
Creativity - Wikipedia — stated that "innovation is more than a new idea or an invention; an innovation requires implementation, either by being put into active use or by being made available
Creativity - Wikipedia — for use by other parties, ﬁrms, individuals, or organizations."[9] erefore, while creativity involves generating new ideas, innovation is about transforming those ideas
Creativity - Wikipedia — into tangible outcomes that have practical applications. e distinction is critical because creativity without implementation remains an idea, whereas innovation leads
Creativity - Wikipedia — to real-world impact.[10]
Creativity - Wikipedia — ere is also emotional creativity,[11] which is described as a paern of cognitive abilities and personality traits related to originality and appropriateness in emotional
Creativity - Wikipedia — experience.[12]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Most ancient cultures, including Ancient Greece,[13] Ancient China, and Ancient India,[14] lacked the concept of creativity, seeing art as a form of discovery and not
Creativity - Wikipedia — creation. e ancient Greeks had no terms for "to create" or "creator" except for the expression poiein (to make), which only applied to poiesis (poetry) and to the poietes
Creativity - Wikipedia — (poet, or "maker", who made it). Plato did not believe in art as a form of creation. He asks in the Republic,[15] "Will we say of a painter that he makes something?" He
Creativity - Wikipedia — answers, "Certainly not, he merely imitates."[13]
Creativity - Wikipedia — It is commonly argued that the notion of "creativity" originated in Western cultures through Christianity, as a maer of divine inspiration.[2] According to scholars, the
Creativity - Wikipedia — earliest Western conception of creativity was the Biblical story of the creation given in Genesis.[14]: 18 However, this is not creativity in the modern sense, which did not
Creativity - Wikipedia — arise until the Renaissance. In the Judeo-Christian-Islamic tradition, creativity was the sole province of God; humans were not considered to have the ability to create
Creativity - Wikipedia — something new except as an expression of God's work.[16] A similar concept existed in Greek culture, where Muses were seen as mediating inspiration from the gods.[17]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Romans and Greeks invoked the concept of an external creative "daemon" (Greek) or "genius" (Latin), linked to the sacred or the divine. However, none of these views
Creativity - Wikipedia — are similar to the modern concept of creativity, and the rejection of creativity in favor of discovery and the belief that individual creation was a conduit of the divine
Creativity - Wikipedia — would dominate the West until the Renaissance and even later.[16][14]: 18–19

Creativity - Wikipedia — It was during the Renaissance that creativity was ﬁrst conceived not as a conduit from the divine, but as arising from the abilities of
Creativity - Wikipedia — "great men."[14]: 18–19 is could be aributed to the leading intellectual movement of the time, aptly named humanism, which developed
Creativity - Wikipedia — an intensely anthropocentric outlook on the world, valuing the intellect and achievement of the individual.[18] From this philosophy
Creativity - Wikipedia — arose the Renaissance man (or polymath), an individual who embodies the principles of humanism in their ceaseless courtship with
Creativity - Wikipedia — knowledge and creation.[19] One of the most well-known and immensely accomplished examples is Leonardo da Vinci.
Creativity - Wikipedia — However, the shi from divine inspiration to the abilities of the individual was gradual and would not become immediately apparent
Creativity - Wikipedia — until the Age of Enlightenment.[14]: 19–21 By the 18th century, creativity (notably in aesthetics) linked with the concept of imagination
Creativity - Wikipedia — became more frequent.[13] In the writing of omas Hobbes, imagination became a key element of human cognition.[2] William Duﬀ
Creativity - Wikipedia — was one of the ﬁrst to identify imagination as a quality of genius, typifying the separation being made between talent (productive, but
Creativity - Wikipedia — not breaking new ground) and genius.[17]
Creativity - Wikipedia — As an independent topic of study, creativity received lile aention until the 19th century.[17] Psychologist Mark Runco and Robert
Creativity - Wikipedia — Albert argue that creativity as the subject of proper study began seriously to emerge in the late 19th century with the increased interest
Creativity - Wikipedia — in individual diﬀerences inspired by the arrival of Darwinism. In particular, they refer to the work of Francis Galton, who, through his
Creativity - Wikipedia — eugenicist outlook, took a keen interest in the heritability of intelligence, with creativity taken as an aspect of genius.[2]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In the late 19th and early 20th centuries, leading mathematicians and scientists such as Hermann von Helmholtz (1896)[20] and Henri Poincaré (1908)[21] began to reﬂect
Creativity - Wikipedia — on and publicly discuss their creative processes. e insights of Poincaré and von Helmholtz were built on early accounts of the creative process by pioneering theorists
Creativity - Wikipedia — such as Graham Wallas and Max Wertheimer. In his work Art of ought, published in 1926,[22] Wallas presented one of the ﬁrst models of the creative process. In the
Creativity - Wikipedia — Wallas model, creative insights and illuminations may be explained by a process consisting of ﬁve stages:
Creativity - Wikipedia — 1. preparation (preparatory work on a problem that focuses the individual's mind on the problem and explores the problem's dimensions),
Creativity - Wikipedia — 2. incubation (in which the problem is internalized into the unconscious mind although nothing appears externally to be happening),
Creativity - Wikipedia — 3. intimation (the creative person gets a "feeling" that a solution is on its way),
Creativity - Wikipedia — 4. illumination or insight (in which the creative idea bursts forth from its preconscious processing into conscious awareness);
Creativity - Wikipedia — 5. veriﬁcation (in which the idea is consciously veriﬁed, elaborated, and then applied).
Creativity - Wikipedia — Wallas's model is also oen treated as four stages, with "intimation" seen as a sub-stage.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Wallas considered creativity to be a legacy of the evolutionary process, which allowed humans to quickly adapt to rapidly changing environments. Simonton[23]
Creativity - Wikipedia — provides an updated perspective on this view in his book, Origins of Genius: Darwinian Perspectives on Creativity.

Creativity - Wikipedia — Modern
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 1927, mathematician and philosopher Alfred North Whitehead gave the Giﬀord Lectures at the University of Edinburgh, later published as Process and Reality.[24] He
Creativity - Wikipedia — is credited with having coined the term "creativity" to serve as the ultimate category of his metaphysical scheme.[25]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Although psychometric studies of creativity had been conducted by e London School of Psychology as early as 1927 with the work of H.L. Hargreaves into the
Creativity - Wikipedia — Faculty of Imagination.[26] e formal psychometric measurement of creativity, from the standpoint of orthodox psychological literature, is usually considered to have
Creativity - Wikipedia — begun with psychologist J.P. Guilford's address to the American Psychological Association in 1950.[27] at address helped to popularize the study of creativity and to
Creativity - Wikipedia — focus aention on scientiﬁc approaches to conceptualizing creativity. Statistical analyzes led to the recognition of creativity as a aspect of human cognition separate
Creativity - Wikipedia — from IQ-type intelligence, under the study of which it had previously been subsumed. Guilford's work suggested that above a threshold level of IQ, the relationship
Creativity - Wikipedia — between creativity and classically measured intelligence broke down.[28]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity is viewed diﬀerently in diﬀerent countries.[29] For example, cross-cultural research centered in Hong Kong found that Westerners view creativity more in
Creativity - Wikipedia — terms of the individual aributes of a person, such as their aesthetic taste, while Chinese people view creativity more in terms of the social inﬂuence of creative people
Creativity - Wikipedia — (i.e. what they can contribute to society).[30] Mpofu, et al., surveyed 28 African languages and found that 27 had no word which directly translated to "creativity", with
Creativity - Wikipedia — Arabic being the exception.[31]: 465 e linguistic relativity hypothesis (i.e. that language can aﬀect thought) suggests that the lack of an equivalent word for "creativity"
Creativity - Wikipedia — may aﬀect the views of creativity among speakers of such languages. However, more research would be needed to establish this, and there is certainly no suggestion
Creativity - Wikipedia — that this linguistic diﬀerence makes people any less—or more—creative. Nevertheless, it is true that there has been very lile research on creativity in Africa[31]: 458 and
Creativity - Wikipedia — Latin America.[32] Creativity has been more thoroughly researched in the northern hemisphere, but there are cultural diﬀerences between northern countries. In
Creativity - Wikipedia — Scandinavia, creativity is seen as an individual aitude that helps people cope with life's challenges,[33] while in Germany, creativity is seen more as a process that can
Creativity - Wikipedia — be applied to help solve problems.[34]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Psychologists James Kaufman and Ronald Begheo introduced a "four C" model of creativity. e four "C's" are:
Creativity - Wikipedia — 1. mini-c ("transformative learning" involving "personally meaningful interpretations of experiences, actions, and insights").
Creativity - Wikipedia — 2. little-c (everyday problem-solving and creative expression).
Creativity - Wikipedia — 3. Pro-C (exhibited by people who are professionally or vocationally creative, though not necessarily eminent).
Creativity - Wikipedia — 4. Big-C (creativity considered great in a given ﬁeld).
Creativity - Wikipedia — is model was intended to help accommodate models and theories of creativity that stressed competence as an essential component and a historic transformation of a
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative domain as the highest mark of creativity. It also, the authors argued, made a useful framework for analyzing creative processes in individuals.[35]

Creativity - Wikipedia — e contrast signiﬁed by the terms "Big C" and "lile C" has been widely used. Kozbelt, Begheo, and Runco used a lile-c/Big-C model to review major theories of
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity.[28] Margaret Boden distinguished between h-creativity (historical) and p-creativity (personal).[36]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Ken Robinson[37] and Anna Cra[38] focused on creativity in a general population, particularly with respect to education. Cra makes a similar distinction between
Creativity - Wikipedia — "high" and "lile c" creativity[38] and cites Robinson as referring to "high" and "democratic" creativity. Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi deﬁned creativity in terms of individuals
Creativity - Wikipedia — judged to have made signiﬁcant creative and perhaps domain-changing contributions.[39] Simonton analyzed the career trajectories of eminent creative people in order

Creativity - Wikipedia — eories of creativity (and empirical investigations of why some people are more creative than others) have focused on a variety of aspects. e dominant factors are
Creativity - Wikipedia — usually identiﬁed as "the four P's," a framework ﬁrst put forward by Mel Rhodes:[41]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Process
Creativity - Wikipedia — A focus on process is shown in cognitive approaches that try to describe thought mechanisms and techniques for creative thinking. Theories
Creativity - Wikipedia — invoking divergent rather than convergent thinking (such as that of Guilford), or those describing the staging of the creative process (such as
Creativity - Wikipedia — that of Wallas) are primarily theories of the creative process.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Product
Creativity - Wikipedia — A focus on a creative product usually attempts to assess creative output, whether for psychometrics (see below) or to understand why some
Creativity - Wikipedia — objects are considered creative. It is from a consideration of product that the standard deﬁnition of creativity as the production of something
Creativity - Wikipedia — both novel and useful arises.[42]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Person
Creativity - Wikipedia — A focus on the nature of the creative person considers more general intellectual habits, such as openness, levels of ideation, autonomy,
Creativity - Wikipedia — expertise, exploratory behavior, and so on.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Press and place
Creativity - Wikipedia — A focus on place (or press) considers the circumstances in which creativity ﬂourishes, such as degrees of autonomy, access to resources, and
Creativity - Wikipedia — the nature of gatekeepers. Creative lifestyles are characterized by nonconforming attitudes and behaviors, as well as ﬂexibility.[43]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 2013, based on a sociocultural critique of the Four-P's model as individualistic, static, and decontextualized, psychology professor and author Vlad Petre Glăveanu
Creativity - Wikipedia — proposed a "Five A's" model consisting of actor, action, artifact, audience, and aﬀordance.[44] In this model, the actor is the person with aributes but who is also located
Creativity - Wikipedia — within social networks; action is the process of creativity not only in internal cognitive terms but also external, bridging the gap between ideation and implementation;
Creativity - Wikipedia — artifacts emphasize how creative products typically represent cumulative innovations over time rather than abrupt discontinuities; and "press/place" is divided into
Creativity - Wikipedia — audience and aﬀordance, which consider the interdependence of the creative individual with the social and material world, respectively. Although not supplanting the
Creativity - Wikipedia — Four P's model in creativity research, the Five A's model has exerted inﬂuence over the direction of some creativity research,[45] and has been credited with bringing
Creativity - Wikipedia — "Four P's" aspects
Creativity - Wikipedia — "Five A's" aspects
Creativity - Wikipedia — coherence to studies across a number of creative domains.[46]
Creativity - Wikipedia — ere has been signiﬁcant research conducted in the ﬁelds of psychology and cognitive science towards beer understanding the processes by which creativity occurs.
Creativity - Wikipedia — e results of these studies have led to several possible explanations of the sources and methods of creativity.[1]
Creativity - Wikipedia — "Incubation" is a temporary break from creative problem solving that can result in insight.[47] Empirical research has investigated whether, as the concept of
Creativity - Wikipedia — "incubation" in Wallas's model implies, a period of interruption or rest from a problem may aid creative problem-solving. Early work proposed that creative solutions to
Creativity - Wikipedia — problems arise mysteriously from the unconscious mind while the conscious mind is occupied with other tasks.[48] is hypothesis is included in Csikszentmihalyi's
Creativity - Wikipedia — ﬁve-phase model of the creative process, which describes incubation as a time when one's unconscious takes over. is was supposed to allow for unique connections to
Creativity - Wikipedia — be made without the conscious mind trying to make logical order out of the problem.[49]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Ward[50] listed various hypotheses that have been advanced to explain why incubation may aid creative problem-solving and notes how some empirical evidence is
Creativity - Wikipedia — consistent with a diﬀerent hypothesis: incubation aids creative problems in that it enables "forgeing" of misleading clues. e absence of incubation may lead the
Creativity - Wikipedia — problem solver to become ﬁxated on inappropriate problem-solving strategies.[51]
Creativity - Wikipedia — J. P. Guilford[52] drew a distinction between convergent and divergent production, or convergent and divergent thinking. Convergent thinking involves aiming for a
Creativity - Wikipedia — single, correct, or best solution to a problem (e.g. "How can we get a crewed rocket to land on the moon safely and within budget?"). Divergent thinking, on the other
Creativity - Wikipedia — hand, involves the creative generation of multiple answers to an open-ended prompt (e.g. "How can a chair be used?").[53] Divergent thinking is sometimes used as a
Creativity - Wikipedia — synonym for creativity in psychological literature or is considered the necessary precursor to creativity.[54] However, as Runco pointed out, there is a clear distinction
Creativity - Wikipedia — between creative thinking and divergent thinking.[53] Creative thinking focuses on the production, combination, and assessment of ideas to formulate something new
Creativity - Wikipedia — and unique, while divergent thinking focuses on conceiving a variety of ideas that are not necessarily new or unique. Other researchers have occasionally used the
Creativity - Wikipedia — terms ﬂexible thinking or ﬂuid intelligence, which are also roughly similar to (but not synonymous with) creativity.[55] While convergent and divergent thinking diﬀer
Creativity - Wikipedia — greatly in terms of approach to problem solving, it is believed that both are employed to some degree in solving most real-world problems.[53]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 1992, Finke, et al., proposed the "Geneplore model", in which creativity takes place in two phases: a generative phase, where an individual constructs mental
Creativity - Wikipedia — representations called "preinventive" structures, and an exploratory phase where those structures are used to come up with creative ideas.[56] Some evidence shows that
Creativity - Wikipedia — when people use their imagination to develop new ideas, those ideas are structured in predictable ways in accordance with properties of existing categories and
Creativity - Wikipedia — concepts.[57] Weisberg argued, in contrast, that creativity involves ordinary cognitive processes yielding extraordinary results.[58]

Creativity - Wikipedia — Helie and Sun[59] proposed a framework for understanding creativity in problem solving, namely the explicit–implicit interaction (EII) theory of creativity. is theory
Creativity - Wikipedia — aempts to provide a more uniﬁed explanation of relevant phenomena (in part by reinterpreting/integrating various fragmentary existing theories of incubation and
Creativity - Wikipedia — insight).
Creativity - Wikipedia — e EII theory relies mainly on ﬁve basic principles:
Creativity - Wikipedia — 1. co-existence of, and the diﬀerence between, explicit and implicit knowledge
Creativity - Wikipedia — 2. simultaneous involvement of implicit and explicit processes in most tasks
Creativity - Wikipedia — 3. redundant representation of explicit and implicit knowledge
Creativity - Wikipedia — 4. integration of the results of explicit and implicit processing
Creativity - Wikipedia — 5. iterative (and possibly bidirectional) processing
Creativity - Wikipedia — A computational implementation of the theory was developed based on the CLARION cognitive architecture and used to simulate relevant human data. is work is an
Creativity - Wikipedia — initial step in the development of process-based theories of creativity, encompassing incubation, insight, and various other related phenomena.
Creativity - Wikipedia — In e Act of Creation, Arthur Koestler introduced the concept of "bisociation" – that creativity arises as a result of the intersection of two quite diﬀerent frames of
Creativity - Wikipedia — reference.[60] In the 1990s, various approaches in cognitive science that dealt with metaphor, analogy, and structure mapping converged, and a new integrative approach
Creativity - Wikipedia — to the study of creativity in science, art, and humor emerged under the label conceptual blending.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Honing theory, developed principally by psychologist Liane Gabora, posits that creativity arises due to the self-organizing, self-mending nature of a worldview. e
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative process is a way by which the individual hones (and re-hones) an integrated worldview. Honing theory places emphasis not only on the externally visible
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative outcome but also on the internal cognitive restructuring and repair of the worldview brought about by the creative process.[61] When one is faced with a
Creativity - Wikipedia — creatively demanding task, there is an interaction between one's conception of the task and one's worldview. e conception of the task changes through interaction
Creativity - Wikipedia — with the worldview, and the worldview changes through interaction with the task. is interaction is reiterated until the task is complete, at which point the task is
Creativity - Wikipedia — conceived of diﬀerently and the worldview is subtly or drastically transformed, following the natural tendency of a worldview to aempt to resolve dissonance and seek
Creativity - Wikipedia — internal consistency amongst its components, whether they be ideas, aitudes, or bits of knowledge. Dissonance in a person's worldview is, in some cases, generated by
Creativity - Wikipedia — viewing their peers' creative outputs, and, so, people pursue their own creative endeavors to restructure their worldviews and reduce dissonance.[61] is shi in
Creativity - Wikipedia — worldview and cognitive restructuring through creative acts has also been considered as a way to explain possible beneﬁts of creativity for mental health.[61] e theory
Creativity - Wikipedia — also addresses challenges not addressed by other theories of creativity, such as the factors guiding restructuring and the evolution of creative works.[62]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A central feature of honing theory is the notion of a "potentiality state".[63] Honing theory posits that creative thought proceeds not by searching through and randomly
Creativity - Wikipedia — "mutating" predeﬁned possibilities but by drawing upon associations that exist due to overlap in the distributed neural-cell assemblies that participate in the encoding of
Creativity - Wikipedia — Explicit–implicit interaction theory
Creativity - Wikipedia — Conceptual blending
Creativity - Wikipedia — Honing theory
Creativity - Wikipedia — experiences in memory. Midway through the creative process, one may have made associations between the current task and previous experiences but not yet
Creativity - Wikipedia — disambiguated which aspects of those previous experiences are relevant to the current task. us, the creative idea may feel "half-baked". At that point, it can be said to
Creativity - Wikipedia — be in a potentiality state, because how it will actualize depends on the diﬀerent internally or externally generated contexts it interacts with.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Honing theory is held to explain certain phenomena not dealt with by other theories of creativity—for example, how diﬀerent works by the same creator exhibit a
Creativity - Wikipedia — recognizable style or "voice" even in diﬀerent creative outlets. is is not predicted by theories of creativity that emphasize chance processes or the accumulation of
Creativity - Wikipedia — expertise, but it is predicted by honing theory, according to which personal style reﬂects the creator's uniquely structured worldview. Another example is the
Creativity - Wikipedia — environmental stimulus for creativity. Creativity is commonly considered to be fostered by a supportive, nurturing, and trustworthy environment conducive to self-
Creativity - Wikipedia — actualization. In line with this idea, Gabora posits that creativity is a product of culture and that our social interactions evolve our culture in way that promotes
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity.[64]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In everyday thought, people oen spontaneously imagine alternatives to reality when they think "if only…".[65] eir counterfactual thinking is viewed as an example of
Creativity - Wikipedia — everyday creative processes.[66] It has been proposed that the creation of counterfactual alternatives to reality depends on cognitive processes that are similar to rational
Creativity - Wikipedia — thought.[67]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Imaginative thought in everyday life can be categorized based on whether it involves perceptual or motor-related mental imagery, novel combinatorial processing, or
Creativity - Wikipedia — altered psychological states. is classiﬁcation aids in understanding the neural foundations and practical implications of imagination.[68]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creative thinking is a central aspect of everyday life, encompassing both controlled and undirected processes. is includes divergent thinking and stage models,
Creativity - Wikipedia — highlighting the importance of extra- and meta-cognitive contributions to imaginative thought.[69]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Brain-network dynamics play a crucial role in creative cognition. e default and executive control networks in the brain cooperate during creative tasks, suggesting a
Creativity - Wikipedia — complex interaction between these networks in facilitating everyday imaginative thought.[70]
Creativity - Wikipedia — e term "dialectical theory of creativity" dates back to psychoanalyst Daniel Dervin[71] and was later developed into an interdisciplinary theory.[72] is theory starts
Creativity - Wikipedia — with the ancient concept that creativity takes place in an interplay between order and chaos. Similar ideas can be found in neuroscience and psychology.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Neurobiologically, it can be shown that the creative process takes place in a dynamic interplay between coherence and incoherence that leads to new and usable
Creativity - Wikipedia — neuronal networks. Psychology shows how the dialectics of convergent and focused thinking with divergent and associative thinking leads to new ideas and products.

Creativity - Wikipedia — Lin and Vartanian developed a neurobiological description of creative cognition.[76] is interdisciplinary framework integrates theoretical principles and empirical
Creativity - Wikipedia — results from neuroeconomics, reinforcement learning, cognitive neuroscience, and neurotransmission research on the locus coeruleus system. It describes how decision-
Creativity - Wikipedia — making processes studied by neuroeconomists as well as activity in the locus coeruleus system underlie creative cognition and the large-scale brain network dynamics
Creativity - Wikipedia — associated with creativity.[77] It suggests that creativity is an optimization and utility maximization problem that requires individuals to determine the optimal way to
Creativity - Wikipedia — exploit and explore ideas (e.g., the multi-armed bandit problem). is utility-maximization process is thought to be mediated by the locus coeruleus system,[78] and this
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity framework describes how tonic and phasic locus coeruleus activities work in conjunction to facilitate the exploiting and exploring of creative ideas. is
Creativity - Wikipedia — framework not only explains previous empirical results but also makes novel and falsiﬁable predictions at diﬀerent levels of analysis (ranging from neurobiological to
Creativity - Wikipedia — cognitive and personality diﬀerences).
Creativity - Wikipedia — B.F. Skinner aributed creativity to accidental behaviors that are reinforced by the environment.[79] In behaviorism, creativity can be understood as novel or unusual
Creativity - Wikipedia — behaviors that are reinforced if they produce a desired outcome.[80] Spontaneous behaviors by living creatures are thought to reﬂect past learned behaviors. In this way,
Creativity - Wikipedia — [81] a behaviorist may say that prior learning caused novel behaviors to be reinforced many times over, and the individual has been shaped to produce increasingly novel
Creativity - Wikipedia — behaviors.[82] A creative person, according to this deﬁnition, is someone who has been reinforced more oen for novel behaviors than others. Behaviorists suggest that
Creativity - Wikipedia — anyone can be creative, they just need to be reinforced to learn to produce novel behaviors.
Creativity - Wikipedia — e "investment theory of creativity" suggests that many individual and environmental factors must exist in precise ways for extremely high, as opposed to average,
Creativity - Wikipedia — levels of creativity to result. In the "investment" sense, a person with their particular characteristics in their particular environment may see an opportunity to devote
Creativity - Wikipedia — their time and energy to something that has been overlooked by others. e creative person develops an undervalued or under-recognized idea to the point where it is
Creativity - Wikipedia — established as a new and creative idea. Just as in the ﬁnancial world, some investments are worth the buy-in, while others are less productive and do not generate
Creativity - Wikipedia — returns to the extent that the investor expected. is "investment theory of creativity" asserts that creativity might rely to some extent on the right investment of eﬀort
Creativity - Wikipedia — being added to a ﬁeld at the right time in the right way.[83][84]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Jürgen Schmidhuber's formal theory of creativity[85] postulates that creativity, curiosity, and interestingness are by-products of a simple computational principle for
Creativity - Wikipedia — measuring and optimizing learning progress.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Consider an agent able to manipulate its environment and thus its own sensory inputs. e agent can use a black box optimization method such as reinforcement
Creativity - Wikipedia — learning to learn, through informed trial and error, sequences of actions that maximize the expected sum of its future reward signals. ere are extrinsic reward signals
Creativity - Wikipedia — for achieving externally given goals, such as ﬁnding food when hungry. But for Schmidhuber's objective function to be maximized also includes an additional, intrinsic
Creativity - Wikipedia — term to model "wow-eﬀects". is non-standard term motivates purely creative behavior of the agent, even when there are no external goals.

Creativity - Wikipedia — Computational creativity
Creativity - Wikipedia — A wow-eﬀect is formally deﬁned as follows: as the agent is creating and predicting and encoding the continually growing history of actions and sensory inputs, it keeps
Creativity - Wikipedia — improving the predictor or encoder, which can be implemented as an artiﬁcial neural network, or some other machine learning device, that can exploit regularities in
Creativity - Wikipedia — the data to improve its performance over time. e improvements can be measured precisely, by computing the diﬀerence in computational costs (storage size, number
Creativity - Wikipedia — of required synapses, errors, time) needed to encode new observations before and aer learning. is diﬀerence depends on the encoder's present subjective knowledge,
Creativity - Wikipedia — which changes over time, but the theory formally takes this into account. e cost diﬀerence measures the strength of the present wow-eﬀect due to sudden
Creativity - Wikipedia — improvements in data compression or computational speed. It becomes an intrinsic reward signal for the action selector. e objective function thus motivates the
Creativity - Wikipedia — action optimizer to create action sequences that cause more wow-eﬀects.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Irregular, random data (or noise) do not permit any wow-eﬀects or learning progress, and thus are "boring" by nature (providing no reward). Already-known and
Creativity - Wikipedia — predictable regularities also are boring. Temporarily interesting are only the initially unknown, novel, regular paerns in both actions and observations. is motivates
Creativity - Wikipedia — the agent to perform continual, open-ended, active, creative exploration.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Schmidhuber's work is highly inﬂuential in intrinsic motivation, which has emerged as a research topic in the study of artiﬁcial intelligence and robotics.
Creativity - Wikipedia — According to Schmidhuber, his objective function explains the activities of scientists, artists, and comedians.[86] For example, physicists are motivated to create
Creativity - Wikipedia — experiments leading to observations that obey previously unpublished physical laws, permiing beer data compression. Likewise, composers receive intrinsic reward
Creativity - Wikipedia — for creating non-arbitrary melodies with unexpected but regular harmonies that permit wow-eﬀects through data compression improvements. Similarly, a comedian
Creativity - Wikipedia — gets an intrinsic reward for "inventing a novel joke with an unexpected punch line, related to the beginning of the story in an initially unexpected but quickly learnable
Creativity - Wikipedia — way that also allows for beer compression of the perceived data."[87]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Schmidhuber augured that computer hardware advances would greatly scale up rudimentary artiﬁcial scientists and artists.[88] He used the theory to create low-
Creativity - Wikipedia — complexity art[89] and an aractive human face.[90]
Creativity - Wikipedia — J. P. Guilford's group,[52] which pioneered the modern psychometric study of creativity, constructed several performance-based tests to measure creativity in 1967,
Creativity - Wikipedia — including asking participants to write original titles for a story with a given plot, asking participants to come up with unusual uses for everyday objects such as bricks,
Creativity - Wikipedia — and asking participants to generate a list of consequences of unexpected events, such as the loss of gravity. Guilford was trying to create a model for intellect as a whole,
Creativity - Wikipedia — but in doing so, he also created a model for creativity. Guilford assumed that creativity was not an abstract concept, which was an important assumption needed for
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity research. e idea that creativity was a category, rather than a single concept, enabled other researchers to look at creativity from a new perspective.[91]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Additionally, Guilford hypothesized one of the ﬁrst models that speciﬁed the components of creativity. He explained that creativity was a result of having three
Creativity - Wikipedia — Personal assessment
Creativity - Wikipedia — Psychometric approaches
Creativity - Wikipedia — History
Creativity - Wikipedia — qualities: the ability to recognize problems, "ﬂuency", and "ﬂexibility". "Fluency" encompassed "ideational ﬂuency", or the ability to rapidly produce a variety of ideas
Creativity - Wikipedia — fulﬁlling stated requirements; "associational ﬂuency", or the ability to generate a list of words associated with a given word; and "expressional ﬂuency", or the ability to
Creativity - Wikipedia — organize words into larger units such as phrases, sentences, and paragraphs. "Flexibility" encompassed both "spontaneous ﬂexibility", or the general ability to be ﬂexible,
Creativity - Wikipedia — and "adaptive ﬂexibility", or the ability to produces responses that are novel and of high quality.
Creativity - Wikipedia — is represents the base model which several researchers would alter to produce their own new theories of creativity years later.[91] Building on Guilford's work, tests
Creativity - Wikipedia — were developed, sometimes called "divergent thinking" (DT) tests, which have been both praised[92] and criticized.[93] One example is the Torrance Tests of Creative
Creativity - Wikipedia — inking developed in 1966.[92] ese test set forth tasks requiring divergent thinking, as well as other problem-solving skills, the tests being scored according to four
Creativity - Wikipedia — categories: "ﬂuency", the total number of meaningful, and relevant, ideas generated; "ﬂexibility", the number of diﬀerent categories of responses; "originality", the
Creativity - Wikipedia — statistical rarity of the responses; and "elaboration", the amount of detail given.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Considerable progress has been made in the automated scoring of divergent-thinking tests, using a semantic approach. When compared to human raters, natural
Creativity - Wikipedia — language processing (NLP) techniques are reliable and valid for the scoring of originality.[94] Computer programs were able to achieve a correlation to human graders of
Creativity - Wikipedia — 0.60 and 0.72.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Semantic networks also devise originality scores that yield signiﬁcant correlations with socio-personal measures.[95] A team of researchers led by James C. Kaufman and
Creativity - Wikipedia — Mark A. Runco combined expertise in creativity research, natural language processing, computational linguistics, and statistical data analysis to devise a scalable system
Creativity - Wikipedia — for computerized automated testing: the SparcIt Creativity Index Testing system. is system enabled automated scoring of DT tests that is reliable, objective, and
Creativity - Wikipedia — scalable, thus addressing most of the issues of DT tests that had been found and reported.[93] e resultant computer system was able to achieve a correlation to human
Creativity - Wikipedia — graders of 0.73.[96]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Researchers have taken a social-personality approach by using personality traits such as independence of judgement, self-conﬁdence, araction to complexity, aesthetic
Creativity - Wikipedia — orientation, and risk-taking as measures of personal creativity.[27] Within the framework of the Big Five personality traits, a consistent few of these traits have emerged
Creativity - Wikipedia — as being correlated to creativity.[97] Openness to experience is consistently related to a host of diﬀerent assessments of creativity.[98] Investigation of the other Big Five
Creativity - Wikipedia — traits has demonstrated subtle diﬀerences between diﬀerent domains of creativity. Compared to non-artists, artists tend to have higher levels of openness to experience
Creativity - Wikipedia — and lower levels of conscientiousness, while scientists are more open to experience, conscientious, and higher in the conﬁdence-dominance facets of extraversion
Creativity - Wikipedia — compared to non-scientists.[99]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Biographical methods use quantitative characteristics, such as the number of publications, patents, or artistic performances that can be credited to a person. While this
Creativity - Wikipedia — method was originally developed for highly creative personalities, today it is also available as self-report questionnaires supplemented with frequent, less outstanding
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative behaviors such as writing a short story or creating recipes. e self-report questionnaire most frequently used in research is the Creative Achievement
Creativity - Wikipedia — estionnaire,[100] a self-report test that measures creative achievement across ten domains, which was described in 2005 and shown to be reliable when compared to
Creativity - Wikipedia — Computer scoring

Creativity - Wikipedia — e potential relationship between creativity and intelligence has been of interest since the last half of the twentieth century, when many inﬂuential studies extensively
Creativity - Wikipedia — studied both. is joint focus highlighted both the theoretical and practical importance of the relationship: researchers were interested in not only if the two qualities
Creativity - Wikipedia — were related, but also how and why.[102]
Creativity - Wikipedia — ere are multiple theories accounting for their relationship, with there being three main theories. reshold theory states that intelligence is a necessary, but not
Creativity - Wikipedia — suﬃcient, condition for creativity, and that there is a moderate positive relationship between creativity and intelligence until IQ ~120.[103][104] Certiﬁcation theory states
Creativity - Wikipedia — that creativity is not intrinsically related to intelligence. Instead, individuals are required to meet the requisite level of intelligence in order to gain a certain level of
Creativity - Wikipedia — education or work, which in turn oﬀers the opportunity to be creative. In this theory, displays of creativity are moderated by intelligence.[105] Interference theory states,
Creativity - Wikipedia — in contrast, that extremely high intelligence might interfere with creative ability.[106]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Sternberg and O'Hara proposed a diﬀerent framework of ﬁve possible relationships between creativity and intelligence: that creativity was a subset of intelligence; that
Creativity - Wikipedia — intelligence was a subset of creativity; that the two constructs overlapped; that they were both part of the same construct (coincident sets); or that they were distinct
Creativity - Wikipedia — constructs (disjoint sets).[107]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A number of researchers include creativity, either explicitly or implicitly, as a key component of intelligence, for example:
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Sternberg's Theory of Successful Intelligence[106][107][108] includes creativity as a main component and comprises three sub-theories: contextual
Creativity - Wikipedia — (analytic), contextual (practical), and experiential (creative). Experiential sub-theory—the ability to use pre-existing knowledge and skills to solve
Creativity - Wikipedia — new and novel problems—is directly related to creativity.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ The Cattell–Horn–Carroll theory (CHC) includes creativity as a subset of intelligence, associated with the broad group factor of long-term
Creativity - Wikipedia — storage and retrieval (Glr).[109] Glr narrows abilities relating to creativity include ideational ﬂuency, associational ﬂuency, and originality/
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity. Silvia et al.[110] conducted a study to look at the relationship between divergent thinking and verbal ﬂuency tests and reported that
Creativity - Wikipedia — both ﬂuency and originality in divergent thinking were signiﬁcantly aﬀected by the broad-level Glr factor. Martindale[111] extended the CHC-
Creativity - Wikipedia — theory by proposing that people who are creative are also selective in their processing speed. Martindale argues that in the creative process,
Creativity - Wikipedia — larger amounts of information are processed more slowly in the early stages, and as a person begins to understand the problem, the
Creativity - Wikipedia — processing speed is increased.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ The Dual Process Theory of Intelligence[112] posits a two-factor or type model of intelligence. Type 1 is a conscious process and concerns goal-
Creativity - Wikipedia — Factors
Creativity - Wikipedia — Intelligence
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity as a subset of intelligence
Creativity - Wikipedia — directed thoughts. Type 2 is an unconscious process, and concerns spontaneous cognition, which encompasses daydreaming and implicit
Creativity - Wikipedia — learning ability. Kaufman argues that creativity occurs as a result of Type 1 and Type 2 processes working together in combination. Each type in
Creativity - Wikipedia — the creative process can be used to varying degrees.
Creativity - Wikipedia — In this relationship model, intelligence is a key component in the development of creativity, for example:
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Sternberg & Lubart's Investment Theory,[83][113] using the metaphor of a stock market, demonstrates that creative thinkers are like good
Creativity - Wikipedia — investors—they buy low and sell high (in their ideas). Like undervalued or low-valued stock, creative individuals generate unique ideas that are
Creativity - Wikipedia — initially rejected by other people. The creative individual has to persevere and convince others of the idea's value. After convincing others, and
Creativity - Wikipedia — thus increasing the idea's value, the creative individual "sells high" by leaving the idea with the other people and moving on to generate
Creativity - Wikipedia — another idea. According to this theory, six distinct, but related elements contribute to successful creativity: intelligence, knowledge, thinking
Creativity - Wikipedia — styles, personality, motivation, and environment. Intelligence is just one of the six factors that can, either solely or in conjunction with the other
Creativity - Wikipedia — ﬁve factors, generate creative thoughts.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Amabile's Componential Model of Creativity[114][115] posits three within-individual components needed for creativity—domain-relevant skills,
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity-relevant processes, and task motivation—and one component external to the individual—their surrounding social environment.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity requires the conﬂuence of all components. High creativity will result when a person is intrinsically motivated, possesses both a high
Creativity - Wikipedia — level of domain-relevant skills and has high skills in creative thinking, and is working in a highly creative environment.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ The Amusement Park Theoretical Model[116] is a four-step theory in which domain-speciﬁc and generalist views are integrated into a model of
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity. The researchers make use of the metaphor of the amusement park to demonstrate that, within each of the following creative levels,
Creativity - Wikipedia — intelligence plays a key role:
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ To get into the amusement park, there are initial requirements (e.g., time and transportation needed to go to the park). Initial requirements
Creativity - Wikipedia — (such as intelligence) are necessary, but not suﬃcient for creativity. They are more like prerequisites for creativity, and if a person does not
Creativity - Wikipedia — possess the basic level of the initial requirement (intelligence), then they will not be able to generate creative thoughts and behaviour.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Secondly, there are the subcomponents—general thematic areas—that increase in speciﬁcity. Like choosing which type of amusement park
Creativity - Wikipedia — to visit (e.g., a zoo or a water park), these areas relate to the areas in which someone could be creative (e.g., poetry).
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Thirdly, there are speciﬁc domains. After choosing the type of park to visit (e.g., if one chooses a waterpark, that person has to choose which
Creativity - Wikipedia — speciﬁc park to go to). For example, within the poetry domain there are many diﬀerent forms (e.g., free verse, riddles, sonnets, etc.).
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Lastly, there are micro-domains. These are the speciﬁc tasks that reside within each domain (e.g., individual rides at the waterpark equate to
Creativity - Wikipedia — individual lines in a poem in free-verse).
Creativity - Wikipedia — Intelligence as a subset of creativity
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity and intelligence as overlapping yet distinct constructs
Creativity - Wikipedia — ese concepts posit creativity and intelligence as distinct, but intersecting constructs, for example:
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ In Renzulli's Three-Ring Conception of Giftedness,[117] giftedness is an overlap of above-average intellectual ability, creativity, and task
Creativity - Wikipedia — commitment. Under this view, creativity and intelligence are distinct constructs, but they overlap under the correct conditions.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ In the PASS theory of intelligence, the planning component—the ability to solve problems, make decisions, and take action—strongly overlaps
Creativity - Wikipedia — with the concept of creativity.[118]
Creativity - Wikipedia — ▪ Threshold Theory (TT) derives from a number of previous research ﬁndings that suggested that a threshold exists in the relationship between
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity and intelligence—both constructs are moderately positively correlated up to an IQ of ~120. Above this threshold, if there is a
Creativity - Wikipedia — relationship at all, it is small and weak.[119][103][120] TT posits that a moderate level of intelligence is necessary for creativity.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Under this view, researchers posit that there are no diﬀerences in the mechanisms underlying creativity from those used in normal problem solving, and in normal
Creativity - Wikipedia — problem solving there is no need for creativity. us, creativity and intelligence (problem solving) are the same thing. Perkins referred to this as the "nothing-special"
Creativity - Wikipedia — view.[121]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In this view, creativity and intelligence are completely diﬀerent, unrelated constructs.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Some theories suggest that creativity may be particularly susceptible to aﬀective inﬂuence. e term "aﬀect" in this context refers to liking or disliking key aspects of
Creativity - Wikipedia — the subject in question. is work largely follows from ﬁndings in psychology regarding the ways in which aﬀective states are involved in human judgment and
Creativity - Wikipedia — decision-making.[122]
Creativity - Wikipedia — According to Alice Isen, positive aﬀect has three primary eﬀects on cognitive activity. First, it makes additional cognitive material available for processing, increasing
Creativity - Wikipedia — the number of cognitive elements available for association. Second, it leads to defocused aention and a more complex cognitive context, increasing the breadth of those
Creativity - Wikipedia — elements that are treated as relevant to the problem. ird, it increases cognitive ﬂexibility, increasing the probability that diverse cognitive elements will in fact become
Creativity - Wikipedia — associated. Together, these processes enable creativity.[123]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Barbara Fredrickson, in her broaden-and-build, model suggests that positive emotions, such as joy and love, broaden a person's available repertoire of cognitions and
Creativity - Wikipedia — actions, thus enhancing creativity.[124]
Creativity - Wikipedia — According to these researchers, positive emotions increase the number of cognitive elements available for association (aention scope) and the number of elements that
Creativity - Wikipedia — are relevant to the problem (cognitive scope). Day-by-day psychological experiences—including emotions, perceptions, and motivation—signiﬁcantly impact creative
Creativity - Wikipedia — performance. Creativity is higher when emotions and perceptions are more positive and when intrinsic motivation is stronger.[125]

Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity and intelligence as disjoint sets

Creativity - Wikipedia — Some meta-analyses, such as Baas, et al., (2008) analyzing 66 studies of creativity and aﬀect, support the link between creativity and positive aﬀect.[126][127]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Links have been identiﬁed between creativity and mood disorders, particularly manic-depressive disorder (a.k.a. bipolar disorder) and depressive disorder (a.k.a.
Creativity - Wikipedia — unipolar disorder).[128] However, diﬀerent artists have described mental illness as having both positive and negative eﬀects on their work.[129] In general, people who
Creativity - Wikipedia — have worked in the arts industry throughout history have faced many environmental factors that are associated with, and can sometimes inﬂuence, mental illness—
Creativity - Wikipedia — things such as poverty, persecution, social alienation, psychological trauma, substance abuse, and high stress.[129]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A study by psychologist J. Philippe Rushton found creativity to correlate with intelligence and psychoticism.[130] Another study found creativity to be greater in people
Creativity - Wikipedia — with schizotypal personality disorder than in people with either schizophrenia or those without mental health disorders.[131][132][133] While divergent thinking was
Creativity - Wikipedia — associated with activation of both sides of the prefrontal cortex, schizotypal individuals were found to have much greater activation of their right prefrontal cortex.[134]
Creativity - Wikipedia — at study hypothesized that such individuals are beer at accessing both hemispheres, allowing them to make novel associations at a faster rate. Consistent with this
Creativity - Wikipedia — hypothesis, ambidexterity is also more common in people with schizotypal personality disorder and schizophrenia. ree studies by Mark Batey and Adrian Furnham
Creativity - Wikipedia — demonstrated the relationships between schizotypal personality disorder,[135] hypomanic personality,[136] and several diﬀerent measures of creativity.
Creativity - Wikipedia — A study of 300,000 persons with schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, or unipolar depression, and their relatives, found overrepresentation in creative professions of those
Creativity - Wikipedia — with bipolar disorder as well as for undiagnosed siblings of those with schizophrenia or bipolar disorder. ere was no overall overrepresentation, but
Creativity - Wikipedia — overrepresentation for artistic occupations, among those diagnosed with schizophrenia. ere was no association for those with unipolar depression or their relatives.
Creativity - Wikipedia — [137]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Another study, involving more than one million people, conducted by Swedish researchers at the Karolinska Institute, reported a number of correlations between
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative occupations and mental illnesses. Writers had a higher risk of anxiety and bipolar disorders, schizophrenia, unipolar depression, and substance abuse, and were
Creativity - Wikipedia — almost twice as likely as the general population to kill themselves. Dancers and photographers were also more likely to have bipolar disorder.[138] ose in the creative
Creativity - Wikipedia — professions were no more likely to have psychiatric disorders than other people, although they were more likely to have a close relative with a disorder, including
Creativity - Wikipedia — anorexia and, to some extent, autism, the Journal of Psychiatric Research reported.[138]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Nancy Andreasen was one of the ﬁrst researchers to carry out a large-scale study of creativity and whether mental illnesses have an impact on someone's ability to be
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative. She expected to ﬁnd a link between creativity and schizophrenia, but her research sample (the book-authors she pooled) had no history of schizophrenia. Her
Creativity - Wikipedia — ﬁndings instead showed that 80% of the creative group previously had some episode of mental illness in their lifetime.[139] When she performed follow-up studies over a
Creativity - Wikipedia — 15-year period, she found that 43% of the authors had bipolar disorder, compared to 1% of the general public.
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 1989 another study, by Kay Redﬁeld Jamison, reaﬃrmed those statistics, with 38% of her sample of authors having a history of mood disorders.[139] Anthony Storr, a
Creativity - Wikipedia — prominent psychiatrist, remarked:
Creativity - Wikipedia — e creative process can be a way of protecting the individual against being overwhelmed by depression, a means of regaining a sense of mastery in those
Creativity - Wikipedia — Mental health
Creativity - Wikipedia — Studies
Creativity - Wikipedia — who have lost it, and, to a varying extent, a way of repairing the self-damaged by bereavement or by the loss of conﬁdence in human relationships which
Creativity - Wikipedia — accompanies depression from whatever cause.[139]
Creativity - Wikipedia — People diagnosed with bipolar disorder report themselves as having a larger range of emotional understanding, heightened states of perception, and an ability to
Creativity - Wikipedia — connect beer with those in the world around them.[140] Other reported traits include higher rates of productivity, higher senses of self-awareness, and greater empathy.
Creativity - Wikipedia — ose who have bipolar disorder also understand their own sense of heightened creativity and ability to get immense numbers of tasks done all at once. In one study, of
Creativity - Wikipedia — 219 participants (aged 19 to 63) diagnosed with bipolar disorder, 82% of them reported having elevated feelings of creativity during their hypomanic swings.[141]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A study done by Shapiro and Weisberg also showed a positive correlation between the manic upswings of the cycles of bipolar disorder and the ability of an individual
Creativity - Wikipedia — to be more creative.[142] e data showed, however, that it was not the depressive swing that brings forth dark creative spurts, but the act of climbing out of the
Creativity - Wikipedia — depressive episode that sparks creativity. e reason behind this spur of creative genius could come from the type of self-image that the person has during a time of
Creativity - Wikipedia — hypomania. A hypomanic person may feel a bolstered sense of self-conﬁdence, creative conﬁdence, and sense of individualism.[142]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Vaitsa Giannouli believes that the creativity a person diagnosed with bipolar disorder feels comes as a form of "stress management".[143] In the realm of music, one
Creativity - Wikipedia — might be expressing one's stress or pains through the pieces one writes in order to beer understand those same feelings. Famous authors and musicians, along with
Creativity - Wikipedia — some actors, would oen aribute their wild enthusiasm to something like a hypomanic state.[144] e artistic side of society has been notorious for behaviors that are
Creativity - Wikipedia — seen as maladapted to societal norms. Symptoms of bipolar disorder correlate with behaviors in high-proﬁle creative personalities such as alcohol addiction; drug abuse
Creativity - Wikipedia — including stimulants, depressants, hallucinogens and dissociatives, opioids, inhalants, and cannabis; diﬃculties in holding regular occupations; interpersonal problems;
Creativity - Wikipedia — legal issues; and a high risk of suicide.[144]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Robert Weisberg believes that the state of mania sets "free the powers of a thinker". He implies that not only has the person become more creative, but they have
Creativity - Wikipedia — fundamentally changed the kind of thoughts they produce.[145] In a study of poets, who are especially highly aﬄicted with bipolar disorders, over a period of three years
Creativity - Wikipedia — those poets would have cycles of creating really creative and powerful works of poetry. e timelines over the three-year study looked at the poets' personal journals
Creativity - Wikipedia — and their clinical records, and found that the timelines between their most powerful poems matched that of their upswings in bipolar disorder.[145]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity can be expressed in a variety of ways, depending on the uniqueness of people and environments. eorists have suggested a number of diﬀerent models of
Creativity - Wikipedia — the creative person. However, the creativity-proﬁling approach must take into account the tension between predicting the creative proﬁle of an individual, as
Creativity - Wikipedia — characterized by the psychometric approach, and the evidence that group creativity is founded on diversity and diﬀerence.[146]
Creativity - Wikipedia — From a personality-traits perspective, there are a number of traits that are associated with creativity in people.[99][147] Creative people tend to be more open to new
Creativity - Wikipedia — experiences, are more self-conﬁdent, are more ambitious, self-accepting, impulsive, driven, dominant, and hostile, compared to people who are less creative.

Creativity - Wikipedia — Personal traits
Creativity - Wikipedia — One characteristic of creative people, as measured by some psychologists, is what is called "divergent production"—the ability of a person to generate a diverse
Creativity - Wikipedia — assortment, yet an appropriate amount, of responses to a given situation.[148] One way to measure divergent production is by administering the Torrance Tests of
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creative inking,[149] which assess the diversity, quantity, and appropriateness of participants' responses to a variety of open-ended questions. Some researchers also
Creativity - Wikipedia — emphasize how creative people are beer at balancing between divergent and convergent production, which depends on an individual's innate preference or ability to
Creativity - Wikipedia — explore and exploit ideas.[76]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Other researchers of creativity see that what distinguishes creative people as a cognitive process of dedication to problem-solving and developing expertise in the ﬁeld
Creativity - Wikipedia — of their creative expression. Hardworking people study the work of people before them in their milieu, become experts in their ﬁelds, and then have the ability to add to
Creativity - Wikipedia — and build upon previous information in innovative and creative ways. In a study of projects by design students, students who had more knowledge of their subject on
Creativity - Wikipedia — average exhibited greater creativity in carrying out their projects.[150]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A person's motivation may also be predictive of their level of creativity. Motivation stems from two diﬀerent sources: intrinsic and extrinsic. Intrinsic motivation is an
Creativity - Wikipedia — internal drive within a person to participate as a result of personal interest, desires, hopes, goals, etc. Extrinsic motivation is a drive from outside and might take the
Creativity - Wikipedia — form of payment, rewards, fame, approval from others, etc. Although intrinsic and extrinsic motivation can both increase creativity in certain cases, strictly extrinsic
Creativity - Wikipedia — motivation oen impedes creativity in people.[115][151]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In studying exceptionally creative people in history, some common traits in lifestyle and environment are oen found. Creative people usually had supportive, but rigid
Creativity - Wikipedia — and non-nurturing, parents. Most had an interest in their ﬁeld at an early age, and most had a highly supportive and skilled mentor in their ﬁeld of interest. Oen the
Creativity - Wikipedia — ﬁeld they chose was relatively uncharted, allowing for their creativity to be expressed more. Most exceptionally creative people devoted almost all of their time and
Creativity - Wikipedia — energy into their cra, and aer about a decade had a creative breakthrough of fame. eir lives were marked with extreme dedication and a cycle of hard-work and
Creativity - Wikipedia — breakthroughs as a result of their determination.[152]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity is a fundamental component of the creative arts and design practice. It allows artists and designers to generate innovative ideas, solve complex problems,
Creativity - Wikipedia — create products and experiences that are meaningful and impactful, stay ahead of trends, and anticipate future needs. Author Austin Kleon asserts that all creative work
Creativity - Wikipedia — builds on what came before. Embracing inﬂuences and educating oneself in the work of others is conducive to creativity.[153]
