Creativity - Wikipedia — e neuroscience of creativity looks at the operation of the brain during creative behavior. One article writes that "creative
Creativity - Wikipedia — innovation might require coactivation and communication between regions of the brain that ordinarily are not strongly
Creativity - Wikipedia — connected."[154] People who excel at creative innovation tend to diﬀer from others in three ways: ﬁrst, they have a high level
Creativity - Wikipedia — of specialized knowledge; second, they are capable of divergent thinking mediated by the frontal lobe; and, third, they are
Creativity - Wikipedia — able to modulate neurotransmiers such as norepinephrine in their frontal lobe.[154] us, the frontal lobe appears to be the
Creativity - Wikipedia — part of the cortex that is most important for creativity.[154][155]
Creativity - Wikipedia — A 2015 study of creativity found that it involves the interaction of multiple neural networks, including those that support
Creativity - Wikipedia — associative thinking, along with other default mode network functions.[156] In 2018, some experiments showed that when the
Creativity - Wikipedia — brain suppresses obvious or "known" solutions, the outcome is solutions that are more creative. is suppression is mediated
Creativity - Wikipedia — by alpha oscillations in the right temporal lobe[157] and activity in the right frontal pole.[155]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity involves the forming of associative elements into new combinations that are useful or meet some requirement. Sleep aids this process.[158][159] REM rather
Creativity - Wikipedia — than NREM sleep appears to be responsible.[160][161] is may be due to changes in cholinergic and noradrenergic neuromodulation that occurs during REM sleep.[160]
Creativity - Wikipedia — During this period of sleep, high levels of acetylcholine in the hippocampus suppress feedback from the hippocampus to the neocortex, and lower levels of acetylcholine
Creativity - Wikipedia — and norepinephrine in the neocortex encourage the spread of associational activity within neocortical areas without control from the hippocampus.[162] is is in
Creativity - Wikipedia — contrast to waking consciousness, during which higher levels of norepinephrine and acetylcholine inhibit recurrent connections in the neocortex. REM sleep may aid
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity by allowing "neocortical structures to reorganize associative hierarchies, in which information from the hippocampus would be reinterpreted in relation to
Creativity - Wikipedia — previous semantic representations or nodes."[160]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Vandervert[163][164] described how the brain's frontal lobes and the cognitive functions of the cerebellum collaborate to facilitate creativity and innovation. Vandervert's
Creativity - Wikipedia — explanation rests on considerable evidence that all processes of working memory (responsible for processing all thought)[165] are adaptively modeled for increased
Creativity - Wikipedia — eﬃciency by the cerebellum.[166][167] e cerebellum (consisting of 100 billion neurons, which is more than the in the entirety of the rest of the brain)[168] also
Creativity - Wikipedia — adaptively models all bodily movement for eﬃciency. e cerebellum's adaptive models of working memory processing are then fed back to especially frontal lobe
Creativity - Wikipedia — working memory control processes,[169] where creative and innovative thoughts arise.[163] (Apparently, creative insight or the "aha" experience is then triggered in the
Creativity - Wikipedia — temporal lobe.)[170]
Creativity - Wikipedia — According to Vandervert, the details of creative adaptation begin in "forward" cerebellar models, which are anticipatory/exploratory controls for movement and
Creativity - Wikipedia — thought. ese cerebellar processing and control architectures have been termed Hierarchical Modular Selection and Identiﬁcation for Control (HMOSAIC).[171] New,
Creativity - Wikipedia — hierarchically-arranged levels of the cerebellar control architecture (HMOSAIC) develop as mental mulling in working memory is extended over time. ese new levels
Creativity - Wikipedia — of the control architecture are fed forward to the frontal lobes. Since the cerebellum adaptively models all movement and all levels of thought and emotion,[167]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Vandervert's approach helps explain creativity and innovation in sports, art, music, the design of video games, technology, mathematics, the child prodigy, and thought
Creativity - Wikipedia — Neuroscience
Creativity - Wikipedia — REM sleep
Creativity - Wikipedia — Vandervert model
Creativity - Wikipedia — in general.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Vandervert argues that when a person is confronted with a challenging new situation, visual-spatial working memory and speech-related working memory are
Creativity - Wikipedia — decomposed and re-composed (fractionated) by the cerebellum and then blended in the cerebral cortex in an aempt to deal with the new situation. With repeated
Creativity - Wikipedia — aempts to deal with challenging situations, the cerebro-cerebellar blending process continues to optimize the eﬃciency of how working memory deals with the
Creativity - Wikipedia — situation or problem.[172] He also argues that this is the same process (only involving visual-spatial working memory and pre-language vocalization) that led to the
Creativity - Wikipedia — evolution of language in humans.[173] Vandervert and Vandervert–Weathers have pointed out that this blending process, because it continuously optimizes eﬃciencies,
Creativity - Wikipedia — constantly improves prototyping aempts toward the invention or innovation of new ideas, music, art, or technology.[174] Prototyping, they argue, not only produces
Creativity - Wikipedia — new products, it trains the cerebro-cerebellar pathways involved to become more eﬃcient at prototyping itself. Furthermore, Vandervert and Vandervert-Weathers
Creativity - Wikipedia — believe that this repetitive "mental prototyping", or mental rehearsal involving the cerebellum and the cerebral cortex, explains the success of the self-driven,
Creativity - Wikipedia — individualized paerning of repetitions initiated by the teaching methods of the Khan Academy.
Creativity - Wikipedia — e model proposed by Vandervert has, however, received incisive critique from several authors.[175]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 2005, Alice Flaherty presented a three-factor model of the creative drive. Drawing from evidence in brain imaging, drug studies, and lesion analysis, she described the
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative drive as resulting from an interaction of the frontal lobes, the temporal lobes, and dopamine from the limbic system. e frontal lobes may be responsible for
Creativity - Wikipedia — idea generation, and the temporal lobes for idea editing and evaluation. Abnormalities in the frontal lobe (such as depression or anxiety) generally decrease creativity,
Creativity - Wikipedia — while abnormalities in the temporal lobe oen increase creativity. High activity in the temporal lobe typically inhibits activity in the frontal lobe, and vice versa. High
Creativity - Wikipedia — dopamine levels increase general arousal and goal-directed behaviors and reduce latent inhibition, with all three eﬀects increasing the drive to generate ideas.[176]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In 2018, Lin and Vartanian proposed a neuroeconomic framework that precisely describes norepinephrine's role in creativity and modulating large-scale brain networks
Creativity - Wikipedia — associated with creativity.[76] is framework describes how neural activity in diﬀerent brain regions and networks, such as the default mode network, track utility or
Creativity - Wikipedia — subjective values of ideas.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Economic approaches to creativity have focused on three aspects – the impact of creativity on economic growth, methods of modeling markets for creativity, and the
Creativity - Wikipedia — maximization of economic creativity (innovation).[177][178]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In the early 20th century, Joseph Schumpeter introduced the economic theory of creative destruction to describe the way in which old ways of doing things are destroyed
Creativity - Wikipedia — and replaced by the new. Some economists (such as Paul Romer) view creativity as an important element in the recombination of elements to produce new technologies
Creativity - Wikipedia — and products and, consequently, economic growth. Creativity leads to capital, and creative products are protected by intellectual property laws.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Mark A. Runco and Daniel Rubenson have tried to describe a "psychoeconomic" model of creativity.[179] In such a model, creativity is the product of endowments and
Creativity - Wikipedia — active investments in creativity; the costs and beneﬁts of bringing creative activity to market determine the supply of creativity. Such an approach has been criticized
Creativity - Wikipedia — Flaherty model
Creativity - Wikipedia — Lin and Vartanian model
Creativity - Wikipedia — Economics
Creativity - Wikipedia — for its view of creativity consumption as always having positive utility, and for the way it prematurely analyzes the value of future innovations.[180]
Creativity - Wikipedia — In his 2002 book, e Rise of the Creative Class, economist Richard Florida popularized the notion that regions with "3 T's of economic development: Technology, Talent,
Creativity - Wikipedia — and Tolerance" also have high concentrations of creative professionals and tend to have a higher level of economic development.[181]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Creativity research for most of the twentieth century was dominated by psychology and business studies, with lile work done in sociology. Since the turn of the
Creativity - Wikipedia — millennium, there has been more aention paid by sociological researchers,[182][183] but sociology has yet to establish creativity as a speciﬁc research ﬁeld, with reviews
Creativity - Wikipedia — of sociological research into creativity a rarity in high-impact literature.[184]
Creativity - Wikipedia — While psychology has tended to focus on the individual as the locus of creativity, sociological research is directed more at the structures and context within which
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative activity takes place, primarily based in sociology of culture, which ﬁnds its roots in the works of Marx, Durkheim, and Weber. is has meant a focus on the
Creativity - Wikipedia — cultural and creative industries as sociological phenomena. Such research has covered a variety of areas, including the economics and production of culture, the role of
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative industries in development, and the rise of the "creative class".[185]
Creativity - Wikipedia — For those who view the conventional system of schooling as stiﬂing creativity, an emphasis is made (particularly in the preschool/kindergarten and early school years)
Creativity - Wikipedia — to provide a creativity-friendly, rich, imagination-fostering environment for young children.[186][187][188] Researchers have seen this as important because technology is
Creativity - Wikipedia — advancing at an unprecedented rate and creative problem-solving will be needed to cope with these challenges as they arise.[188] In addition to helping with problem
Creativity - Wikipedia — solving, creativity also helps students identify problems where others have failed to do so.[186][187][189] e Waldorf School is an example of an education program that
Creativity - Wikipedia — promotes creative thought.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Promoting intrinsic motivation and problem solving are two areas where educators can foster creativity in students. Students are more creative when they see a task as
Creativity - Wikipedia — intrinsically motivating, valued for its own sake.[187][188][190][191] To promote creative thinking, educators need to identify what motivates their students and to
Creativity - Wikipedia — structure teaching around it. Providing students with a choice of activities allows them to become more intrinsically motivated and therefore creative in completing the
Creativity - Wikipedia — tasks.[186][192]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Teaching students to solve problems that do not have well-deﬁned answers is another way to foster their creativity. is is accomplished by allowing students to explore
Creativity - Wikipedia — problems and redeﬁne them, possibly drawing on knowledge that at ﬁrst may seem unrelated to the problem in order to solve it.[186][187][188][190] In adults, mentoring
Creativity - Wikipedia — individuals is another way to foster their creativity.[193] However, the beneﬁts of mentoring creativity apply only to creative contributions considered great in a given
Creativity - Wikipedia — ﬁeld, not to everyday creative expression.[194]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Musical creativity is a gateway to the ﬂow state, which is conducive to spontaneity, improvisation, and creativity. Studies show that it is beneﬁcial to emphasize
Creativity - Wikipedia — students' creative side and integrate more creativity into their curriculums, with a notable strategy being through music.[195] One reason for this is that students are able
Creativity - Wikipedia — to express themselves through musical improvisation in a way that taps into higher order brain regions, while connecting with their peers and allowing them to go
Creativity - Wikipedia — beyond typical paern generation.[196] In this sense, improvisation is a form of self-expression that can generate connectivity between peers and surpass the age-old

Creativity - Wikipedia — Education
Creativity - Wikipedia — rudimentary aspects of school.
Creativity - Wikipedia — In the Scoish education system, creativity is identiﬁed as a core skillset for learning, life, and work, and is deﬁned as "a process which generates ideas that have value
Creativity - Wikipedia — to the individual. It involves looking at familiar things with a fresh eye, examining problems with an open mind, making connections, learning from mistakes, and using
Creativity - Wikipedia — imagination to explore new possibilities."[197] e need to develop a shared language and understanding of creativity and its role across every aspect of learning,
Creativity - Wikipedia — teaching, and continuous improvement was identiﬁed as a necessary aim;[198] and a set of four skills is used to allow educators to discuss and develop creativity across
Creativity - Wikipedia — all subjects and sectors of education – curiosity, open-mindedness, imagination, and problem solving.[199] Distinctions are made between creative learning (when
Creativity - Wikipedia — learners are using their creativity skills), creative teaching (when educators are using their own creativity skills), and creative change (when creativity skills are applied
Creativity - Wikipedia — to planning and improvement). Scotland's national Creative Learning Plan[200] supports the development of creativity skills in all learners and of educators' expertise in
Creativity - Wikipedia — developing creativity skills. A range of resources has been created to support and assess this, including a national review of creativity learning by Her Majesty's
Creativity - Wikipedia — Inspectorate for Education.[197]
Creativity - Wikipedia — China recognizes that creativity is crucial for national security, social development, and generally beneﬁing the people. Measures have been proposed to enhance
Creativity - Wikipedia — creative ability in the country.[201]
Creativity - Wikipedia — e European Union sees creativity as important for the development of basic skills, and has declared 2009 the Year of Creativity and Innovation. Countries such as
Creativity - Wikipedia — France, Germany, Italy, and Spain have made the encouragement of creativity a part of their educational and economic policies.[202]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Various research studies set out to establish that organizational eﬀectiveness depends to a large extent on the creativity of the workforce. For any given organization,
Creativity - Wikipedia — measures of eﬀectiveness vary, depending upon the organization's mission, environmental context, nature of work, the product or service it produces, and customer
Creativity - Wikipedia — demands. us, the ﬁrst step in evaluating organizational eﬀectiveness is to understand the organization itself – how it functions, how it is structured, and what it
Creativity - Wikipedia — emphasizes.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Similarly, social psychologists, organizational scientists, and management scientists (who research factors that inﬂuence creativity and innovation in teams and
Creativity - Wikipedia — organizations) have developed integrative theoretical models that emphasize the elements of team composition, team processes, and organizational culture. ese
Creativity - Wikipedia — theoretical models also emphasize the mutually reinforcing relationships between those elements in promoting innovation.[203][204][205][206]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Research studies of the knowledge economy may be classiﬁed into three levels: macro, meso, and micro. Macro studies are at a societal or transnational level. Meso
Creativity - Wikipedia — studies focus on organizations. Micro investigations center on the working of workers. ere is also an interdisciplinary dimension when researching business,[207]

Creativity - Wikipedia — Supportive and motivational environments that create psychological safety, encourage risk-taking, and tolerate mistakes
Creativity - Wikipedia — increase team creativity.[203][204][205][206] Organizations in which help-seeking, help-giving, and collaboration are rewarded
Creativity - Wikipedia — promote innovation by providing opportunities and contexts in which team processes that lead to collective creativity can
Creativity - Wikipedia — occur.[214] Additionally, leadership styles that downplay hierarchies or power diﬀerences within an organization, and
Creativity - Wikipedia — empower people to speak up about their ideas or opinions, also help to create cultures that are conducive to creativity.[203]
Creativity - Wikipedia — [204][205][206]
Creativity - Wikipedia — e diversity of team members' backgrounds and knowledge can increase team creativity by expanding the collection of
Creativity - Wikipedia — unique information that is available to the team and by introducing diﬀerent perspectives that can be integrated in novel
Creativity - Wikipedia — ways. However, under some conditions, diversity can also decrease team creativity by making it more diﬃcult for team members to communicate about ideas and
Creativity - Wikipedia — causing interpersonal conﬂicts between those with diﬀerent perspectives.[215] us, the potential advantages of diversity must be supported by appropriate team
Creativity - Wikipedia — processes and organizational cultures in order to enhance creativity.[203][204][205][206][216][217]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Team communication norms, such as respecting others' expertise, paying aention to others' ideas, expecting information sharing, tolerating disagreements,
Creativity - Wikipedia — negotiating, remaining open to others' ideas, learning from others, and building on each other's ideas, increase team creativity by facilitating the social processes
Creativity - Wikipedia — involved with brainstorming and problem solving. rough these processes, team members can access their collective pool of knowledge, reach shared understandings,
Creativity - Wikipedia — identify new ways of understanding problems or tasks, and make new connections between ideas. Engaging in these social processes also promotes positive team aﬀect,
Creativity - Wikipedia — which facilitates collective creativity.[203][205][206][216]
Creativity - Wikipedia — ere is a long-standing debate on how material constraints (e.g., lack of money, materials, or equipment) aﬀect creativity. In psychological and managerial research,
Creativity - Wikipedia — there are two competing views. In one view, scholars propose a negative eﬀect of material constraints on innovation and claim that material constraints starve
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity.[218] Proponents argue that adequate material resources are needed to engage in creative activities such as experimenting with new solutions and idea
Creativity - Wikipedia — exploration.[218] In an opposing view, scholars assert that people tend to stick to established routines or solutions as long as they are not forced to deviate from them by
Creativity - Wikipedia — constraints.[219] For example, material constraints facilitated the development of jet engines in World War II.[220]
Creativity - Wikipedia — To reconcile these competing views, contingency models were proposed.[221][222][223] e rationale behind these models is that certain contingency factors (e.g.,
Creativity - Wikipedia — Organizational culture
Creativity - Wikipedia — Team composition
Creativity - Wikipedia — Team processes
Creativity - Wikipedia — Constraints
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity climate or creativity-relevant skills) inﬂuence the relationship between constraints and creativity.[221] ese contingency factors reﬂect the need for higher
Creativity - Wikipedia — levels of motivation and skills when working on creative tasks under constraints.[221] Depending on these contingency factors, there is either a positive or negative
Creativity - Wikipedia — relationship between constraints and creativity.[221][222]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Several researchers have proposed methods of increasing a person's creativity. Such ideas range from the psychological-cognitive—such as the Osborn-Parnes Creative
Creativity - Wikipedia — Problem Solving Process, Synectics, science-based creative thinking, Purdue Creative inking Program, and Edward de Bono's lateral thinking—to the highly structured
Creativity - Wikipedia — —such as TRIZ (the eory of Inventive Problem-Solving) and its variant Algorithm of Inventive Problem Solving (developed by the Russian scientist Genrich
Creativity - Wikipedia — Altshuller), and Computer-Aided morphological analysis.
Creativity - Wikipedia — An empirical synthesis, of which methods work best in enhancing creativity, was published by Haase et al.[224] Summarising the results of 84 studies, the authors found
Creativity - Wikipedia — that complex training courses, meditation, and cultural exposure were most eﬀective in enhancing creativity, while the use of cognitive-manipulation drugs was
Creativity - Wikipedia — noneﬀective.[224]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Experiments suggest the need for closure of task participants, whether as a reﬂection of personality or induced (through time pressure), negatively impacts creativity.
Creativity - Wikipedia — [225] Accordingly, it has been suggested that reading ﬁction, which can reduce the cognitive need for closure, may encourage creativity.[226]
Creativity - Wikipedia — "Malevolent creativity" is the "dark side" of creativity.[227][228] is type of creativity is not typically accepted within society and is deﬁned by the intention to cause
Creativity - Wikipedia — harm to others through original and innovative means. While it is oen associated with criminal behavior, it can also be observed in ordinary day-to-day life as lying,
Creativity - Wikipedia — cheating, and betrayal.[229]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Malevolent creativity should be distinguished from negative creativity in that negative creativity may unintentionally cause harm to others, whereas malevolent
Creativity - Wikipedia — creativity is malevolently motivated.
Creativity - Wikipedia — Malevolent creativity is a key contributor to crime and in its most destructive form can even manifest as terrorism. As creativity requires deviating from the
Creativity - Wikipedia — conventional, there is permanent tension between being creative and going too far—in some cases to the point of breaking the law. Aggression is a key predictor of
Creativity - Wikipedia — malevolent creativity, and increased levels of aggression correlate with a higher likelihood of commiing crime.[230]

Creativity - Wikipedia — Psychology portal
Creativity - Wikipedia — Although everyone shows some levels of malevolent creativity under certain conditions, those that have a higher propensity towards it have increased tendencies to
Creativity - Wikipedia — deceive and manipulate others for their own gain. While malevolent creativity appears to dramatically increase when an individual is treated unfairly, personality,
Creativity - Wikipedia — particularly aggressiveness, is also a key predictor in anticipating levels of malevolent thinking. Researchers Harris and Reiter-Palmon investigated the role of
Creativity - Wikipedia — aggression in levels of malevolent creativity, in particular levels of implicit aggression and the tendency to employ aggressive actions in response to problem solving.
Creativity - Wikipedia — e personality traits of physical aggression, conscientiousness, emotional intelligence, and implicit aggression all seem to be related with malevolent creativity.[228]
Creativity - Wikipedia — Harris and Reiter-Palmon's research showed that when subjects were presented with a problem that designed to trigger malevolent creativity, participants high in
Creativity - Wikipedia — implicit aggression and low in premeditation expressed the largest number of malevolently themed solutions. When presented with the more benign problem designed
Creativity - Wikipedia — to trigger prosocial motives of helping others and cooperating, those high in implicit aggression, even if they tended to be highly impulsive, were far less destructive in
Creativity - Wikipedia — their imagined solutions. e researchers concluded premeditation, more than implicit aggression, controlled an individual's expression of malevolent creativity.[231]
Creativity - Wikipedia — e current measure for malevolent creativity is the 13-item Malevolent Creativity Behaviour Scale (MCBS).[229]

Fine art - Wikipedia — Fine art
Fine art - Wikipedia — In European academic traditions, ﬁne art (or, ﬁne arts) is made primarily for aesthetics or creative expression,
Fine art - Wikipedia — distinguishing it from popular art, decorative art or applied art, which also either serve some practical function (such as
Fine art - Wikipedia — poery or most metalwork) or is generally of limited artistic quality in order to appeal to the masses. In the aesthetic theories
Fine art - Wikipedia — developed in the Italian Renaissance, the highest art was that which allowed the full expression and display of the artist's
Fine art - Wikipedia — imagination,[1] unrestricted by any of the practical considerations involved in, say, making and decorating a teapot. It was
Fine art - Wikipedia — also considered important that making the artwork did not involve dividing the work between diﬀerent individuals with
Fine art - Wikipedia — specialized skills, as might be necessary with a piece of furniture, for example.[2] Even within the ﬁne arts, there was a
Fine art - Wikipedia — hierarchy of genres based on the amount of creative imagination required, with history painting placed higher than still life.
Fine art - Wikipedia — Historically, the ﬁve main ﬁne arts were painting, sculpture, architecture, music, and poetry. Other "minor or subsidiary arts"
Fine art - Wikipedia — were also included, especially performing arts such as theatre and dance, which were counted as "among the most ancient
Fine art - Wikipedia — and universal."[3] In practice, outside education, the concept is typically only applied to the visual arts. e old master print
Fine art - Wikipedia — and drawing were included as related forms to painting, just as prose forms of literature were to poetry. Today, the range of
Fine art - Wikipedia — what would be considered ﬁne arts (in so far as the term remains in use) is commonly includes additional modern forms,
Fine art - Wikipedia — such as ﬁlm, photography, and video production/editing, as well as traditional forms made in a ﬁne art seing, such as studio
Fine art - Wikipedia — poery and studio glass, with equivalents in other materials.
Fine art - Wikipedia — One deﬁnition of ﬁne art is "a visual art considered to have been created primarily for aesthetic and intellectual purposes and
Fine art - Wikipedia — judged for its beauty and meaningfulness, speciﬁcally, painting, sculpture, drawing, watercolor, graphics, and architecture."[4]
Fine art - Wikipedia — In that sense, there are conceptual diﬀerences between the ﬁne arts and the decorative arts or applied arts (these two terms
Fine art - Wikipedia — covering largely the same media). As far as the consumer of the art was concerned, the perception of aesthetic qualities required a reﬁned judgment usually referred to
Fine art - Wikipedia — as having good taste, which diﬀerentiated ﬁne art from popular art and entertainment.[5]
Fine art - Wikipedia — e word "ﬁne" does not so much denote the quality of the artwork in question, but the purity of the discipline according to traditional European canons.[7] Except in
Fine art - Wikipedia — the case of architecture, where a practical utility was accepted, this deﬁnition originally excluded the "useful" applied or decorative arts, and the products of what were
Fine art - Wikipedia — regarded as cras. In contemporary practice, these distinctions and restrictions have become essentially meaningless, as the concept or intention of the artist is given
Fine art - Wikipedia — primacy, regardless of the means through which it is expressed.[8]

Fine art - Wikipedia — of East Asia. e set of "ﬁne arts" are sometimes also called the "major arts", with "minor arts" equating to the decorative arts. is would typically be for medieval and

Fine art - Wikipedia — According to some writers, the concept of a distinct category of ﬁne art is an invention of the early modern period in the
Fine art - Wikipedia — West. Larry Shiner in his e Invention of Art: A Cultural History (2003) locates the invention in the 18th century: "ere was
Fine art - Wikipedia — a traditional "system of the arts" in the West before the eighteenth century. (Other traditional cultures still have a similar
Fine art - Wikipedia — system.) In that system, an artist or artisan was a skilled maker or practitioner, a work of art was the useful product of skilled
Fine art - Wikipedia — work, and the appreciation of the arts was integrally connected with their role in the rest of life. "Art", in other words, meant
Fine art - Wikipedia — approximately the same thing as the Greek word "techne", or in English "skill", a sense that has survived in phrases like "the
Fine art - Wikipedia — art of war", "the art of love", and "the art of medicine".[9] Similar ideas have been expressed by Paul Oskar Kristeller, Pierre
Fine art - Wikipedia — Bourdieu, and Terry Eagleton (e.g. e Ideology of the Aesthetic), though the point of invention is oen placed earlier, in the
Fine art - Wikipedia — Italian Renaissance; Anthony Blunt notes that the term arti di disegno, a similar concept, emerged in Italy in the mid-16th
Fine art - Wikipedia — century.[10]
Fine art - Wikipedia — But it can be argued that the classical world, from which very lile theoretical writing on art survives, in practice had similar
Fine art - Wikipedia — distinctions. e names of artists preserved in literary sources are Greek painters and sculptors, and to a lesser extent the
Fine art - Wikipedia — carvers of engraved gems. Several individuals in these groups were very famous, and copied and remembered for centuries
Fine art - Wikipedia — aer their deaths. e cult of the individual artistic genius, which was an important part of the Renaissance theoretical basis
Fine art - Wikipedia — for the distinction between "ﬁne" and other art, drew on classical precedent, especially as recorded by Pliny the Elder. Some
Fine art - Wikipedia — other types of object, in particular Ancient Greek poery, are oen signed by their makers or the owner of the workshop,
Fine art - Wikipedia — probably partly to advertise their products.
Fine art - Wikipedia — e decline of the concept of "ﬁne art" is dated by George Kubler and others to around 1880. When it "fell out of fashion" as,
Fine art - Wikipedia — by about 1900, folk art was also coming to be regarded as signiﬁcant.[11] Finally, at least in circles interested in art theory,
Fine art - Wikipedia — ""ﬁne art" was driven out of use by about 1920 by the exponents of industrial design … who opposed a double standard of
Fine art - Wikipedia — judgment for works of art and for useful objects".[12] is was among theoreticians; it has taken far longer for the art trade
Fine art - Wikipedia — and popular opinion to catch up. However, over the same period of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, the movement of
Fine art - Wikipedia — prices in the art market was in the opposite direction, with works from the ﬁne arts drawing much further ahead of those
Fine art - Wikipedia — from the decorative arts.

Fine art - Wikipedia — by all other cultures. But traditional Chinese art had comparable distinctions, distinguishing within Chinese painting
Fine art - Wikipedia — between the mostly landscape literati painting of scholar gentlemen and the artisans of the schools of court painting and
Fine art - Wikipedia — sculpture. Although high status was also given to many things that would be seen as cra objects in the West, in particular
Fine art - Wikipedia — ceramics, jade carving, weaving, and embroidery, this by no means extended to the workers who created these objects, who
Fine art - Wikipedia — typically remained even more anonymous than in the West. Similar distinctions were made in Japanese and Korean art. In
Fine art - Wikipedia — Islamic art, the highest status was generally given to calligraphy, architects and the painters of Persian miniatures and
Fine art - Wikipedia — related traditions, but these were still very oen court employees. Typically they also supplied designs for the best Persian
Fine art - Wikipedia — carpets, architectural tiling and other decorative media, more consistently than happened in the West.

Fine art - Wikipedia — itself inspired by the Constructivist Movement, which reunited arts with cras based upon socialist principles. In Africa,

Fine art - Wikipedia — composed of what would ordinarily be considered in the West to be cra production. Some of its most admired
Fine art - Wikipedia — manifestations, such as textiles, fall in this category.
Fine art - Wikipedia — Painting as a ﬁne art means applying paint to a ﬂat surface (as opposed for example to painting a sculpture, or a piece of
Fine art - Wikipedia — poery), typically using several colours. Prehistoric painting that has survived was applied to natural rock surfaces, and wall
Fine art - Wikipedia — painting, especially on wet plaster in the fresco technique was a major form until recently. Portable paintings on wood panel
Fine art - Wikipedia — or canvas have been the most important in the Western world for several centuries, mostly in tempera or oil painting. Asian
Fine art - Wikipedia — painting has more oen used paper, with the monochrome ink and wash painting tradition dominant in East Asia. Paintings
Fine art - Wikipedia — that are intended to go in a book or album are called "miniatures", whether for a Western illuminated manuscript or in
Fine art - Wikipedia — Persian miniature and its Turkish equivalent, or Indian paintings of various types. Watercolour is the western version of painting in paper; forms using gouache, chalk,
Fine art - Wikipedia — and similar mediums without brushes are really forms of drawing.

Fine art - Wikipedia — brushes, wax color pencils, crayons, charcoals, chalk, pastels, markers, stylus, or various metals like silverpoint. ere are a number of subcategories of drawing,

Fine art - Wikipedia — Printmaking covers the making of images on paper that can be reproduced multiple times by a printing process. It has been an important artistic medium for several
Fine art - Wikipedia — centuries, in the West and East Asia. Major historic techniques include engraving, woodcut and etching in the West, and woodblock printing in East Asia, where the
Fine art - Wikipedia — Japanese ukiyo-e style is the most important. e 19th-century invention of lithography and then photographic techniques have partly replaced the historic techniques.
Fine art - Wikipedia — Older prints can be divided into the ﬁne art Old Master print and popular prints, with book illustrations and other practical images such as maps somewhere in the
Fine art - Wikipedia — middle.
Fine art - Wikipedia — Except in the case of monotyping, the process is capable of producing multiples of the same piece, which is called a print. Each print is considered an original, as
Fine art - Wikipedia — opposed to a copy. e reasoning behind this is that the print is not a reproduction of another work of art in a diﬀerent medium – for instance, a painting – but rather
Fine art - Wikipedia — an image designed from inception as a print. An individual print is also referred to as an impression. Prints are created from a single original surface, known technically
Fine art - Wikipedia — as a matrix. Common types of matrices include: plates of metal, usually copper or zinc for engraving or etching; stone, used for lithography; blocks of wood for
Fine art - Wikipedia — woodcuts, linoleum for linocuts and fabric in the case of screen-printing. But there are many other kinds. Multiple nearly identical prints can be called an edition. In
Fine art - Wikipedia — modern times each print is oen signed and numbered forming a "limited edition". Prints may also be published in book form, as artist's books. A single print could be
Fine art - Wikipedia — the product of one or multiple techniques.

Fine art - Wikipedia — through their architectural achievements. Such buildings as the pyramids of Egypt and the Roman Colosseum are cultural symbols, and are important links in public
Fine art - Wikipedia — consciousness, even when scholars have discovered much about past civilizations through other means. Cities, regions, and cultures continue to identify themselves
Fine art - Wikipedia — with, and are known by, their architectural monuments.[18]

Fine art - Wikipedia — Sculpture is three-dimensional artwork created by shaping hard or plastic material, commonly stone (either rock or marble), metal, or wood. Some sculptures are created
Fine art - Wikipedia — directly by carving; others are assembled, built up and ﬁred, welded, molded, or cast. Because sculpture involves the use of materials that can be moulded or modulated,
Fine art - Wikipedia — it is considered one of the plastic arts. e majority of public art is sculpture. Many sculptures together in a garden seing may be referred to as a sculpture garden.
Fine art - Wikipedia — Sculpture in stone survives far beer than works of art in perishable materials, and oen represents the majority of the surviving works (other than poery) from
Fine art - Wikipedia — ancient cultures; conversely, traditions of sculpture in wood may have vanished almost entirely. However, most ancient sculpture was brightly painted, and this has
Fine art - Wikipedia — been lost.[19]

Fine art - Wikipedia — Conceptual art is art in which the concept(s) or idea(s) involved in the work take precedence over traditional aesthetic and
Fine art - Wikipedia — material concerns. e inception of the term in the 1960s referred to a strict and focused practice of idea-based art that oen
Fine art - Wikipedia — deﬁed traditional visual criteria associated with the visual arts in its presentation as text. However, through its association
Fine art - Wikipedia — with the Young British Artists and the Turner Prize during the 1990s, its popular usage, particularly in the UK, developed as a
Fine art - Wikipedia — synonym for all contemporary art that does not practice the traditional skills of painting and sculpture.[20]

Fine art - Wikipedia — qualities of timbre and texture (which are sometimes termed the "color" of a musical sound). Diﬀerent styles or types of music may emphasize, de-emphasize or omit
Fine art - Wikipedia — some of these elements.

Fine art - Wikipedia — venue, usually a building, for viewing such movies. Films are produced by recording images from the world with cameras, or
Fine art - Wikipedia — by creating images using animation techniques or special eﬀects. Films are cultural artifacts created by speciﬁc cultures,
Fine art - Wikipedia — which reﬂect those cultures, and, in turn, aﬀect them. Film is considered to be an important art form, a source of popular
Fine art - Wikipedia — entertainment and a powerful method for educating – or indoctrinating – citizens. e visual elements of cinema give motion
Fine art - Wikipedia — pictures a universal power of communication. Some ﬁlms have become popular worldwide aractions by using dubbing or
Fine art - Wikipedia — subtitles that translate the dialogue.

Fine art - Wikipedia — photography, though many additional issues arise when both the camera and elements of the scene may be in motion.

Fine art - Wikipedia — ▪ Origami – The last century has witnessed a renewed interest in understanding the behavior of folding matter
Fine art - Wikipedia — with contributions from artists and scientists. Origami is diﬀerent from other arts: while painting requires the addition of matter, and sculpture

Fine art - Wikipedia — its time, with a bloodline ending in technology and spacecraft. Its computational aspect and shareable quality (empowered by social networks)
Fine art - Wikipedia — are parts of the puzzle that is making origami a paradigmatic art of the 21st century.[23][24][25]

Work of art - Wikipedia — A work of art, artwork,[1] art piece, piece of art or art object is an artistic creation of
Work of art - Wikipedia — aesthetic value. Except for "work of art", which may be used of any work regarded as art
Work of art - Wikipedia — in its widest sense, including works from literature and music, these terms apply
Work of art - Wikipedia — principally to tangible, physical forms of visual art:
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ An example of ﬁne art, such as a painting or sculpture.
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ Objects in the decorative arts or applied arts that have been designed for
Work of art - Wikipedia — aesthetic appeal, as well as any functional purpose, such as a piece of
Work of art - Wikipedia — jewellery, many ceramics and much folk art.
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ An object created for principally or entirely functional, religious or other
Work of art - Wikipedia — non-aesthetic reasons which has come to be appreciated as art (often later,
Work of art - Wikipedia — or by cultural outsiders).
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ A non-ephemeral photograph or ﬁlm.
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ A work of installation art or conceptual art.
Work of art - Wikipedia — Used more broadly, the term is less commonly applied to:
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ A ﬁne work of architecture or landscape design
Work of art - Wikipedia — ▪ A production of live performance, such as theater, ballet, opera,
Work of art - Wikipedia — performance art, musical concert and other performing arts, and other
Work of art - Wikipedia — ephemeral, non-tangible creations.

Work of art - Wikipedia — issues and philosophies. e term objet d'art is reserved to describe works of art that are
Work of art - Wikipedia — not paintings, prints, drawings or large or medium-sized sculptures, or architecture (e.g.
Work of art - Wikipedia — household goods, ﬁgurines, etc., some purely aesthetic, some also practical). e term
Work of art - Wikipedia — oeuvre is used to describe the complete body of work completed by an artist throughout a career.[2]

Work of art - Wikipedia — A work of art in the visual arts is a physical two- or three- dimensional object that is
Work of art - Wikipedia — professionally determined or otherwise considered to fulﬁll a primarily independent
Work of art - Wikipedia — aesthetic function. A singular art object is oen seen in the context of a larger art
Work of art - Wikipedia — movement or artistic era, such as: a genre, aesthetic convention, culture, or regional-
Work of art - Wikipedia — national distinction.[3] It can also be seen as an item within an artist's "body of work" or
Work of art - Wikipedia — oeuvre. e term is commonly used by museum and cultural heritage curators, the
Work of art - Wikipedia — interested public, the art patron-private art collector community, and art galleries.[4]
Work of art - Wikipedia — Physical objects that document immaterial or conceptual art works, but do not conform to
Work of art - Wikipedia — artistic conventions, can be redeﬁned and reclassiﬁed as art objects. Some Dada and Neo-
Work of art - Wikipedia — Dada conceptual and readymade works have received later inclusion. Also, some
Work of art - Wikipedia — architectural renderings and models of unbuilt projects, such as by Vitruvius, Leonardo da
Work of art - Wikipedia — Vinci, Frank Lloyd Wright, and Frank Gehry, are other examples.
Work of art - Wikipedia — e products of environmental design, depending on intention and execution, can be
Work of art - Wikipedia — "works of art" and include: land art, site-speciﬁc art, architecture, gardens, landscape
Work of art - Wikipedia — architecture, installation art, rock art, and megalithic monuments.
